## Q1-r1

arm `folder`, model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, wall 9.6 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Frequency: across all five years, what percentage of cases have base section 16(h), and what percentage have base section 20(a)(2)? Round to one decimal.
```

```
You are a careful analyst. You can use the Read, Grep, Glob and Bash tools (Bash has Python 3). Your working directory is the only place you may look for files; use only files in it. Show the number or answer each question asks for in your final reply.
```

```
Of the 9,958 cases, **39.6%** have base section 16(h) and **22.9%** have base section 20(a)(2).

I got these by running `python3 reference/tools/cases.py group --by base --top 15` from the folder root. Each case is one row, and the denominator is all 9,958 rows.

| Base section | Cases | Share of 9,958 |
|---|---|---|
| 16(h) | 3,941 | 39.58%, which rounds to 39.6% |
| 20(a)(2) | 2,284 | 22.94%, which rounds to 22.9% |

- **Blank cite:** one row has a blank cite. I kept it in the denominator, since the question asks about all cases. Dropping it would not change either rounded figure.
- **16(i):** the orders print 16(i) for 2025 damages, which may describe the same duty as 16(h) under a later lettering scheme. I did not merge them, as you didn't ask. Those 240 cases are 2.4% of all cases.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Across all five order years (2021–2025):

- **Base section 16(h):** 3,941 of 9,958 cases, which is **39.6%** (39.58% before rounding).
- **Base section 20(a)(2):** 2,284 of 9,958 cases, which is **22.9%** (22.94% before rounding).

I counted each recommendation form as one case, as the definitions say. The base section keeps any qualifier out, so 16(h) includes cites like "16(h)(expire)". The 20(a)(2) count is separate from "20(a)(3)(b)", which is its own base section.

I ran this with `python3 reference/tools/cases.py count --base "16(h)"` and the same command with `--base "20(a)(2)"`. Example cases for 16(h) are 32960 (ord_PSD_011525.pdf, p25), 28736 (ord_PSD_060623.pdf, p80) and 27424 (ord_PSD_122822.pdf, p167). Examples for 20(a)(2) are 32551 (ord_PSD_011525.pdf, p19), 26147 (ord_PSD_060822.pdf, p338) and 27309 (ord_PSD_122822.pdf, p174).

The orders use 16(h) for older damages and 16(i) for 2025 damages. The 2025 law text appears to list the same duty under the later letter, which is an inference. I did not merge 16(i) into 16(h), so the 16(h) share could be understated.
```

```
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
```

```
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
```

## Q4-r2

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Warnings: what percentage of utility-respondent cases, and what percentage of non-utility-respondent cases, have a recommendation containing the words "warning letter" (any capitalization)? Round to one decimal.
```

## Q5-r2

arm `folder`, model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, wall 8.7 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Training option: among cases with a headline penalty above $0, what percentage have a recommendation that contains the word "training" (any capitalization)? Round to one decimal.
```

```
You are a careful analyst. You can use the Read, Grep, Glob and Bash tools (Bash has Python 3). Your working directory is the only place you may look for files; use only files in it. Show the number or answer each question asks for in your final reply.
```

```
def is_utility(respondent):
    """Respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of UTILITY_PREFIXES."""
    s = (respondent or "").lstrip().casefold()
    return any(s.startswith(p.casefold()) for p in UTILITY_PREFIXES)


def load(path=None):
    path = path or DEFAULT_CSV
    with open(path, newline="", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        rows = [enrich(r) for r in csv.DictReader(f)]
    for r in rows:
        r["page"] = int(r["page"])
    return rows
```

```
**187 distinct normalized respondents have 5 or more cases. Together they account for 1,760 of the 7,243 non-utility-respondent cases, which is 24.3%.**

- **Data and denominator:** I used all 9,958 rows in `reference/cases.csv` (58 orders, 2021-01-01 to 2025-12-31). 7,243 of them are non-utility respondents under your 13-prefix list, so that is the denominator.
- **Normalization:** I applied your steps in order. Lowercase, delete characters that are not letters, digits or spaces, collapse and trim spaces, then drop one final whole word if it is inc, llc, co, corp, corporation or company.
- **Calculation:** 1,760 / 7,243 = 24.2997%, which rounds to 24.3%.
- **How I computed it:** I wrote a short Python script that reads the CSV directly instead of using the folder's `cases.py` tool. I did this so your exact normalization order would be applied. I did not re-run the tool, so I have not compared its output with mine.
- **Largest groups:** "miller pipeline" has 91 cases, "riethriley construction co" has 54, and "calumet civil contractors" has 48. "riethriley construction co" keeps its "co" because the trailing "co" is only dropped from names that end in it after the first normalization, and here "co" is followed by nothing else, so I should flag it: my script dropped it only when it was the last word, and the printed key shows "co" remaining, which means the original name ended in a different word such as "Co., Inc."
- **Caveat:** Spelling variants of one company are not merged, so the 187 and the 24.3% are floors. The one row with a blank respondent counts as a non-utility case but forms no group of 5.
```

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.
```

```
Who gets hit: among non-utility-respondent cases, which "other party" name appears most often (exact text, ignoring case and surrounding spaces), and in what percentage of non-utility-respondent cases? Round to one decimal.
```

```
**Indianapolis has the most cases, with 1,153. The five most common cities together account for 26.2% of the cases that have a city.**

I computed this directly from `reference/cases.csv`, using one row per case. The city is the text after the last comma of `damage_location`, trimmed and lowercased. Spelling variants are not merged.

- **Cases with a city:** 9,943 of 9,958. The other 15 have no comma or an empty city and are left out.
```

```
| City | Cases |
|---|---|
| Indianapolis | 1,153 |
| Fort Wayne | 529 |
| Evansville | 445 |
| South Bend | 295 |
| Bloomington | 184 |

- **Percentage:** the five together are 1,153 + 529 + 445 + 295 + 184 = 2,606 cases. That is 2,606 of 9,943, or 26.2% to one decimal.
- **Next city:** Elkhart is sixth with 179 cases, close behind Bloomington.

I did not run this through the folder's `cases.py` tool, so there is no tool receipt or sample case list for these counts.
```

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Reporting lag: for each case with a parseable damage date (month/day/year), define the **lag** as the number of calendar days from the damage date to the order date. Using the order's year (not the damage year), what is the mean lag in days for cases in 2023 and in 2024, each rounded to the nearest whole day, and what percentage of all such cases (all years) have a lag of more than 365 days? Round the percentage to one decimal.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
- **Mean lag in 2023:** 227 days. That is 479,000 total days over 2,107 cases, which gives 227.34.
- **Mean lag in 2024:** 188 days. That is 356,003 total days over 1,897 cases, which gives 187.67.
- **Share of cases with a lag over 365 days:** 12.6%. That is 1,259 of the 9,957 cases with a parseable damage date, or 12.64%.

Year is the order's year, and lag is the order date minus the damage date in calendar days. One case has no parseable damage date and is excluded from the denominator. It is case 24409 in order ord_PSD_022322.pdf, page 129. The tool's own percentage, 12.64%, divides 1,259 by all 9,958 cases. I used 9,957 as the question's definition requires, and both round to 12.6%.

I got these from the tool in `reference/tools/cases.py`, run on `reference/cases.csv`. The 2023 and 2024 figures use `stat --field lag_days --year <year>`. The over-365 count uses `count` with the filter `r['lag_days']!='' and r['lag_days']>365`.
```

```
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
```

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.
```

```
Qualifier outcomes: a case has qualifier "expire", "prior" or "out" if its cite ends with that word in parentheses, on any base section. Among cases with each of those three qualifiers separately, what percentage have a headline penalty of $0? Round each to one decimal.
```

```
| Qualifier | Cases with $0 headline | All cases with the qualifier | Percentage |
|---|---|---|---|
| expire | 185 | 518 | **35.7%** |
| prior | 59 | 272 | **21.7%** |
| out | 35 | 191 | **18.3%** |

I got the counts from the folder's tool, `reference/tools/cases.py`, run over all 9,958 cases. For each qualifier I ran `count --qual <q>` and then `count --qual <q> --zero-penalty`. I did the division myself and rounded to one decimal.

A case counts as $0 when its recommendation has no "$" amount, for example a warning letter or training only. I did not check the tool's parsing against the raw order text.
```

```
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).

- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
```

```
**Answer**

| | 2023 | 2024 |
|---|---|---|
| (a) Non-utility cases with headline penalty ≥ $5,000 | **41.5%** (623 of 1,502) | **48.3%** (687 of 1,421) |
| (b) Utility respondent cases as a share of all cases | **28.7%** (605 of 2,107) | **25.1%** (476 of 1,897) |

From 2023 to 2024, the share of non-utility cases at $5,000 or more rose by about 6.8 percentage points. The utility share of all cases fell by about 3.6 points.

**Reading taken:** "At least $5,000" is inclusive (≥ 5000). Years are order years. Every case is one row, and $0 cases stay in the denominators.

**Commands:** I ran these from the folder root with `python3 reference/tools/cases.py count`. The filters were `--year 2023` or `--year 2024`, then `--utility no`, `--utility no --penalty-min 5000`, or `--utility yes`.

The tool's output also shows each count as a share of all 9,958 rows. I ignored those and computed the percentages above from the counts.

| Year | All cases | Non-utility | Non-utility ≥ $5,000 | Utility |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2023 | 2,107 | 1,502 | 623 | 605 |
| 2024 | 1,897 | 1,421 | 687 | 476 |

**Caveats:**
- These are cases approved in each order year, not damages that occurred in that year.
- I didn't run the scan-pages check (`--scan no`). Scan pages carry most of the field errors, so respondent names could shift a few cases between utility and non-utility.
- I didn't pull example case receipts for these counts.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Answer (by order year, counting each case form as one case)**

| | 2023 | 2024 | Shift |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Non-utility cases with headline penalty ≥ $5,000 | 623 of 1,502 = **41.5%** | 687 of 1,421 = **48.3%** | +6.8 points |
| (b) Utility respondent cases as a share of all cases | 605 of 2,107 = **28.7%** | 476 of 1,897 = **25.1%** | −3.6 points |

- **Denominators:** They include $0 cases. The 2023 totals reconcile (605 + 1,502 = 2,107), and so do the 2024 totals (476 + 1,421 = 1,897).
- **How I got the counts:** I ran `python3 reference/tools/cases.py count --year Y --utility yes|no [--penalty-min 5000]` for Y = 2023 and 2024. I then did the percentage division by hand.
- **Reading taken:** "At least $5,000" is inclusive (≥ 5,000). I did not run the exclusive reading.
- **Caveats:** Years are order-date batches, so a change between them is not a change in dig damage. The headline penalty is the first dollar figure in the recommendation, not necessarily a fine that was paid. I did not rerun with scan pages excluded.
```
